The [Martin Marietta X-23A PRIME][ref_x23] was six feet eight inches long, weighed 894 pounds, and was thrown at the Pacific Ocean three times from the top of an Atlas. On the third attempt, on 19 April 1967, it flew 710 statute miles to one side of the path a stone would have taken, deployed a parachute, and was caught in mid-air by a transport aeroplane near Kwajalein. **It is the smallest vehicle in this series and it answered a larger question than most of the aircraft that dwarf it.** This article is the twenty-fourth in the [X-Planes series][related_post_a297_xplanes_framing], following the [X-1][related_post_a298_bell_x1], the [X-2][related_post_a299_bell_x2], the [X-3][related_post_a300_douglas_x3], the [X-4][related_post_a301_northrop_x4], the [X-5][related_post_a302_bell_x5], the [X-6][related_post_a303_convair_x6], the [X-7][related_post_a304_lockheed_x7], the [X-8][related_post_a305_aerojet_x8], the [X-9][related_post_a306_bell_x9], the [X-10][related_post_a307_north_american_x10], the [X-11][related_post_a308_convair_x11], the [X-12][related_post_a309_convair_x12], the [X-13][related_post_a310_ryan_x13], the [X-14][related_post_a311_bell_x14], the [X-15][related_post_a312_north_american_x15], the [X-16][related_post_a313_bell_x16], the [X-17][related_post_a314_lockheed_x17], the [X-18][related_post_a315_hiller_x18], the [X-19][related_post_a316_curtiss_wright_x19], the [X-20][related_post_a317_boeing_x20], the [X-21][related_post_a318_northrop_x21], and the [X-22][related_post_a319_bell_x22].

**There is also a difficulty with the number, and it is not a small one.** The designation X-23A is attached to this vehicle by encyclopaedias, by museums, and by the historical literature. Available United States Air Force nomenclature records show that it was never assigned, and further that it was never even requested. This article treats the vehicle and the paperwork as two separate subjects, because they are, and because the second one is evidence about how the designation system actually worked and not a clerical footnote.

## The Research Question

**Can a vehicle returning from orbit choose where it lands?**

That is the question, and in 1964 it was genuinely open. A ballistic capsule cannot choose. It falls where the atmosphere and its own entry conditions put it, and the only control available is when to start. The landing point is therefore wherever the orbital ground track happens to be at the moment of the deorbit burn, and if that is the middle of an ocean then that is where the vehicle goes.

The alternative was known in principle. A body that generates lift as well as drag can hold itself up, glide, and by rolling that lift vector to one side, turn. **The open question was not whether this worked but how much of it a returning vehicle could actually buy**, because the lift available to a shape that must also survive entry heating is very small. A lifting body of the period had a hypersonic lift-to-drag ratio somewhere near one, against roughly seventeen for a transport aeroplane at cruise. The ratio is the one the whole article turns on and it is simply

$$\frac{L}{D} = \frac{C_L}{C_D}$$

so a value near unity means a vehicle that produces as much drag as lift. **The lifting body gives up a factor of about fourteen against a transport wing**, and the question is what the remainder buys.

**The keystone of this article is the conversion of that small lift-to-drag ratio into lateral distance**, which the period literature calls lateral range and the modern literature calls crossrange, in [Garland and Hall 1958][research_garland_hall_1958], [Jackson 1961][research_jackson_1961], [Baradell 1962][research_baradell_1962], [Donald L Baradell and Charles H McLellan 1963][research_donald_l_baradell_charles_h_mclellan_1963], [Kornreich 1963][research_kornreich_1963], [Wang 1963, APPROXIMATE SOLUTIONS OF THE LATE][research_wang_1963], [Wang and Skulsky 1963][research_wang_skulsky_1963], [Lessing et al 1964][research_lessing_1964], [Ashford 1965][research_ashford_1965], [Wagner 1965][research_wagner_1965], [Holloway and Pritchard 1967][research_holloway_pritchard_1967], [Lau 1967][research_lau_1967], [Pritchard and Holloway 1967][research_pritchard_holloway_1967], [Holloway 1968][research_holloway_1968], [Niemann 1968][research_niemann_1968], [NlEMANN 1968][research_nlemann_1968], [Moote 1970][research_moote_1970], [Bump 1977][research_bump_1977], [Vinh et al 1977][research_vinh_1977], [Johnson et al 1982][research_johnson_1982], [Kelley et al 1982][research_kelley_1982]. Every part of the vehicle was dimensioned against it. The shape exists to produce the lift, the ablator exists to let the vehicle stay in the atmosphere long enough to use it, the reaction controls and flaps exist to hold the bank angle that converts it, and the recovery system exists so that the measurement survives.

The [X-20 Dyna-Soar][related_post_a317_boeing_x20] had asked a version of this question and had been cancelled in December 1963 without flying. PRIME asked the narrowest possible form of it, without a pilot, for a small fraction of the money.

## Programme Origin

PRIME was the second half of a United States Air Force programme called START, for Spacecraft Technology and Advanced Reentry Tests, begun in 1964. The first half was [ASSET][ref_asset], which flew earlier and addressed structures and heating on a small unmanned glider. **PRIME addressed manoeuvring**, which ASSET had not.

The institutional context is the cancellation of Dyna-Soar. The Air Force had spent years and a great deal of money on a piloted, winged, manoeuvring re-entry vehicle and had been left with the question unanswered and the answer still wanted. START was the cheap route to the same information. Two small unmanned vehicles, launched on existing boosters, instrumented and recovered, could measure heating and manoeuvring without a pilot, without a runway, and without a programme large enough to be worth cancelling.

Martin Marietta supplied the airframe. The company had been developing a family of lifting-body shapes under the designation SV-5, and the family is the reason three vehicles appear here where the title names one. The SV-5D was the small unmanned entry vehicle that became PRIME. The SV-5P was the larger piloted low-speed vehicle that became the [X-24A][ref_x24]. The two are the same aerodynamic idea at different sizes and different ends of the speed range, which is exactly why the designation question became tangled.

## The Vehicle

The SV-5D was a blunted lifting body with a rounded delta planform, two upper fins, and flaps at the rear. It was 6 feet 8 inches long, 3 feet 10 inches across, 2 feet 10 inches high, and weighed 894 pounds at entry.

**Almost the whole of it was thermal protection.** The structure was titanium, beryllium, stainless steel and aluminium, and over it went an ablative heat shield between 0.75 and 2.75 inches thick, with a carbon phenolic nose cap. Attitude was held by nitrogen gas reaction jets and by aerodynamic flaps, which took over as the air became thick enough to be useful.

One quantity that the analysis below needs is not published. **The reference area of the SV-5D does not appear in the accessible record**, so it is derived here from the X-24A, which is the same shape at larger scale and whose reference area is published as 195 square feet against a length of 24.5 feet and a span of 13.64 feet. The ratio of area to the length-times-span box is

$$k_S = \frac{S_\text{X-24A}}{l\,b} = \frac{195}{24.5 \times 13.64} = 0.5835$$

and applying that ratio to the SV-5D's own length and span gives

$$S = k_S\, l\, b = 0.5835 \times 2.032 \times 1.168 = 1.385\ \text{m}^2$$

or 14.9 square feet. **This is an assumption and every number that depends on it inherits that status.** The crossrange result does not depend on it at all, which is stated here because it matters.

One check on the scaling is worth doing because it fails, and the failure is informative. The two vehicles are in a linear ratio of

$$\frac{l_\text{SV-5D}}{l_\text{X-24A}} = \frac{2.032}{7.47} = 0.272$$

so if they were the same thing at different sizes their weights would stand as the cube of that, or 0.020. The actual ratio against the X-24A's 11,450 pounds is

$$\frac{894}{11{,}450} = 0.078$$

**Nearly four times what geometric scaling predicts.** The reason is that the SV-5D is very nearly solid thermal protection while the X-24A is mostly enclosed volume with a pilot and a rocket engine inside it. **Areas scale and contents do not**, which is why the reference area derived above is defensible and a weight derived the same way would not have been. Estimating what a vehicle will weigh, and knowing which parts of it obey a scaling law and which do not, is a methodical discipline and not a guess, in [Carreyette 1950][research_carreyette_1950], ## Sizing From First Principles

### The Force Balance That Makes a Glide an Equilibrium

A vehicle at orbital speed needs no lift, because the centrifugal effect of travelling that fast round a curved Earth supports it entirely. At rest it needs lift equal to its full weight. In between, an equilibrium glide is one that holds the difference.

Written out, the radial balance carries three terms rather than two, and the middle one is the effect the previous sentence names,

$$L\cos\phi + \frac{m V^2}{R} = W$$

Writing $\bar V = V/V_c$ for speed as a fraction of circular satellite speed, and $\phi$ for the angle through which the lift vector has been rolled away from vertical, and using $V_c^2 = gR$ to turn the centrifugal term into $W\bar V^2$, the balance collapses to

$$L\cos\phi = W\left(1 - \bar V^2\right)$$

which says that the lift a gliding vehicle needs is its weight less whatever its own speed is already carrying. At $\bar V = 1$ it needs none. At three quarters of circular speed it needs

$$1 - 0.75^2 = 0.4375$$

of its weight, and at entry, where $\bar V = 0.972$, it needs 0.056 of it. **The glide therefore begins almost unloaded**, which is worth holding on to because it explains the deceleration figures further down.

The circular speed itself follows from equating gravitational and centripetal acceleration at the surface, giving

$$V_c = \sqrt{g R} = \sqrt{9.80665 \times 6.371\times 10^6} = 7{,}904\ \text{m/s}$$

which is 25,933 feet per second. The equilibrium glide is the reference trajectory of the whole subject, and its literature is [Foss and Whitcomb 1960][research_foss_whitcomb_1960], [Moe 1960][research_moe_1960], [Wang and Ting 1961][research_wang_ting_1961], [Bate and Johnson 1962][research_bate_johnson_1962], [Hankey 1963][research_hankey_1963], [Hankey and Hooks 1963][research_hankey_hooks_1963], [Stern and Chu 1963][research_stern_chu_1963], [Ruger 1964][research_ruger_1964], [Approximate analysis of atmosphere re-entry trajectories II 1965][research_approximate_analysis_1965], [Bell 1965][research_bell_1965], [Hooks and Hankey 1965][research_hooks_hankey_1965], [V. A. Yaroshevskii Approximate 1965][research_v_a_1965], [Dolan et al 1966][research_dolan_1966], [Interplanetary guidance systems requirements 1966][research_interplanetary_guidance_1966], [Laporte 1966][research_laporte_1966], [Delpino et al 1967][research_delpino_1967], [Dix et al 1967][research_dix_1967], [Krusos 1967][research_krusos_1967], [Elsey and Carlson 1968][research_elsey_carlson_1968], [Fong et al 1970][research_fong_1970], [Morth 1970][research_morth_1970], [Randall 1970][research_randall_1970], [Crawford 1971][research_crawford_1971], [Mc Namara 1971][research_mc_namara_1971], [Pyle 1971][research_pyle_1971], [Weiss 1971][research_weiss_1971], [Arrington and Woods 1972][research_arrington_woods_1972], [Kelley and Sullivan 1972][research_kelley_sullivan_1972], [Rehder and Holloway 1972][research_rehder_holloway_1972], [Young et al 1972][research_young_1972], [Treybig 1975][research_treybig_1975], [Chern and Vinh 1978][research_chern_vinh_1978], [Townend 1979][research_townend_1979], [Chern and Vinh 1980][research_chern_vinh_1980], [Letts and Castle 1981][research_letts_castle_1981], [Vinh et al 1981][research_vinh_1981], [Greene and Williamson 1982][research_greene_williamson_1982], [Wurster 1983][research_wurster_1983], [Ohya and Kato 1985][research_ohya_kato_1985], [Tillier 1998][research_tillier_1998], [Desai et al 1999, Entry Trajectory Issues for the S][research_desai_1999], [Desai et al 1999, Entry Trajectory Issues for the S][research_desai_1999_2], [Bryant 2002][research_bryant_2002], [Shen et al 2002][research_shen_2002], [Rea and Putnam 2007][research_rea_putnam_2007].

### Why the Turn Rate Does Not Depend on the Atmosphere

Two further statements complete the mechanics. Drag decelerates the vehicle along its path, and the sideways component of the rolled lift vector turns it,

$$m\frac{dV}{dt} = -D, \qquad m V \frac{d\psi}{dt} = L\sin\phi$$

where $\psi$ is the heading. Dividing the second by the first eliminates time, and substituting the force balance eliminates the weight, leaving

$$\frac{d\psi}{dV} = -\frac{L}{D}\,\frac{\sin\phi}{V}$$

**Every property of the vehicle except its lift-to-drag ratio has dropped out, and so has the atmosphere.** Mass, size, reference area and air density are all gone. That is the reason this analysis can be done at all for a vehicle whose reference area is not published. Integrating from the entry speed gives

$$\psi(V) = \frac{L}{D}\sin\phi \, \ln\frac{V_e}{V}$$

The heading change is logarithmic in the speed ratio, which means the great majority of the turning happens late, when the vehicle is slow. **None of this is original to this article.** The equilibrium glide and the relations that follow from it are standard material, set out in [Vinh's treatment of atmospheric flight trajectories][book_vinh_1981] and in [Regan and Anandakrishnan on re-entry dynamics][book_regan_anandakrishnan_1993]. What is done here is to carry them through to a requirement and then to invert them on a measurement. At a lift-to-drag ratio of 1.2 and a bank of 45 degrees, slowing from circular speed to a quarter of it turns the vehicle through

$$\psi = 1.2 \times \sin 45^\circ \times \ln 4 = 1.176\ \text{rad} = 67.4^\circ$$

### The Crossrange Integral

Crossrange accumulates at $\dot y = V\sin\psi$. The classical treatment replaces $\sin\psi$ by $\psi$ at this point, which is what makes a closed form available at all. **That step is examined below and it turns out to cost about a tenth of the answer**, but it is kept here because it is the step the period literature took and the closed form it produces is the relation the subject is built on. Dividing the lateral rate by the deceleration removes time here as well,

$$\frac{dy}{dV} = -\,\frac{V\psi\,(L/D)\cos\phi}{g\left(1-\bar V^2\right)}$$

and the substitution $u = \bar V^2$, for which

$$V = V_c\sqrt{u}, \qquad dV = \frac{V_c}{2\sqrt{u}}\,du, \qquad g = \frac{V_c^2}{R}$$

makes every factor of $V_c$ cancel, leaving

$$dy = -\frac{R}{2}\,\frac{L}{D}\cos\phi\,\frac{\psi\,du}{2\left(1-u\right)}$$

Substituting the heading solution, in which $\ln(V_e/V) = \tfrac{1}{2}\ln(u_e/u)$, turns the accumulated lateral distance into a single integral,

$$y = \frac{R}{4}\left(\frac{L}{D}\right)^2 \sin\phi\cos\phi \; I(u_e), \qquad I(u_e) = \int_0^{u_e} \frac{\ln(u_e/u)}{1-u}\,du$$

For entry at exactly circular speed the integral has a closed form, and it is worth doing rather than quoting. Expand the denominator as a geometric series,

$$\frac{1}{1-u} = \sum_{n\ge 0} u^n \qquad \text{for} \quad 0 \le u < 1$$

and integrate term by term. Each term is a standard integral,

$$\int_0^1 (-\ln u)\,u^n\,du = \frac{1}{(n+1)^2}$$

so the sum is over the reciprocal squares, which is the Basel series,

$$I(1) = \int_0^1 \frac{-\ln u}{1-u}\,du = \sum_{k\ge 1} \frac{1}{k^2} = \frac{\pi^2}{6} = 1.6449$$

**A constant from elementary number theory turns up in the range of a re-entry vehicle**, which is a coincidence of the integral and not a fact about spacecraft, but it is a pleasing one.

### The Bank Angle Is Already Decided

The only free choice in the expression is $\phi$, and it appears as

$$\sin\phi\cos\phi = \tfrac{1}{2}\sin 2\phi$$

Differentiating and setting the result to zero gives the condition, and the second derivative confirms which kind of stationary point it is,

$$\frac{d}{d\phi}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\sin 2\phi\right) = \cos 2\phi = 0, \qquad \frac{d^2}{d\phi^2} = -2\sin 2\phi = -2 < 0$$

so the stationary point is a maximum and it sits at

$$\phi_\text{opt} = 45^\circ, \qquad \sin\phi\cos\phi = \tfrac{1}{2}$$

**This does not depend on the lift-to-drag ratio, on the entry speed, or on anything about the vehicle.** Any vehicle wanting maximum crossrange banks 45 degrees, and the guidance system's apparently central decision turns out to have been made in advance by the arithmetic.

Putting the optimum and the closed form together gives the governing relation of the whole subject,

$$\frac{y_\text{max}}{R} = \frac{\pi^2}{48}\left(\frac{L}{D}\right)^2 = 0.2056\left(\frac{L}{D}\right)^2$$

**Crossrange goes as the square of the lift-to-drag ratio.** Worked at a ratio of 1.2, for a vehicle entering at circular speed,

$$y_\text{max} = 0.2056 \times 1.2^2 \times 6.371\times 10^6 = 1.886\times 10^6\ \text{m} = 1{,}019\ \text{nmi}$$

and the same arithmetic gives 1,310 kilometres at a ratio of 1.0 and 2,568 at 1.4. A ballistic capsule at zero gets nothing, and that is not a small number made small but a capability that is simply absent.

### What the Small-Angle Step Costs

The closed form above was checked against a numerical integration that keeps $\sin\psi$ exactly, and **the two disagree by more than a rounding error.**

The reason is visible in the heading solution, which can simply be evaluated at the end of the glide. Taking that to be one percent of circular speed,

$$\psi_\text{end} = \frac{L}{D}\sin 45^\circ \ln\frac{V_e}{0.01\,V_c}$$

which gives 148 degrees at a ratio of 0.8, **185 degrees at 1.0**, 222 at 1.2 and 260 at 1.4. **Beyond 180 degrees $\sin\psi$ does not merely differ from $\psi$, it has the opposite sign.** The approximation does not degrade gracefully. It inverts.

There is a related point that the exact treatment makes precise. Lateral displacement accumulates as $\dot y = V\sin\psi$, so it stops accumulating exactly where the heading passes half a turn,

$$\sin\psi = 0 \quad \Longrightarrow \quad \psi = 180^\circ, \qquad V_{180} = V_e\exp\left(-\frac{\pi}{(L/D)\sin\phi}\right)$$

At a ratio of 1.2 that speed is 189 metres per second, which is very near the end of the flight. **Crossrange is therefore maximised at the moment the vehicle finishes turning round**, and everything after that is spent flying back toward the plane it started in.

| Lift-to-drag ratio | Closed form | Exact | Over-prediction |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0.8 | 838 km | 797 km | 5.2 percent |
| 1.0 | 1,310 km | 1,214 km | 7.9 percent |
| 1.2 | 1,886 km | 1,696 km | 11.2 percent |
| 1.4 | 2,568 km | 2,233 km | 15.0 percent |

**The classical relation is optimistic, and increasingly so as the lift-to-drag ratio rises**, because a better gliding vehicle turns further and the approximation is worse the further it turns. Both numbers are given from here on.

There is a physical statement hiding in that table. **A vehicle holding 45 degrees of bank through a full lifting entry turns through half a circle.** It arrives pointing the way it came. Real entry guidance reverses the bank part way through for exactly this reason, and the constant-bank assumption that makes the mathematics tractable is not a trajectory anyone would fly.

### What the Requirement Actually Is

Now the other half. **How much crossrange does a returning vehicle need?**

Successive orbits do not retrace the same ground. The Earth turns underneath, so each pass lays its track to the west of the last by the rotation accumulated in one orbital period. The rate wanted here is the rotation relative to the fixed stars, because the orbit plane is fixed in that frame and not in the one that tracks the Sun,

$$\omega_E = \frac{2\pi}{86{,}164\ \text{s}} = 7.292\times 10^{-5}\ \text{rad/s}$$

Using the solar day of 86,400 seconds instead would overstate the interval by 0.27 percent, which is small but is an avoidable error. The track spacing in longitude is then

$$\Delta\lambda = \omega_E T = 7.292\times 10^{-5} \times 5400 = 0.3937\ \text{rad} = 22.56^\circ$$

for a nominal ninety-minute low orbit. The geometry of ground tracks, and of the orbits that lay them down, is standard astrodynamics, in [Bosanquet 1958][research_bosanquet_1958], [Ambrosio et al 1961][research_ambrosio_1961], [Musen and Squires 1965][research_musen_squires_1965], [Alderson 1967][research_alderson_1967], [Endres 1967][research_endres_1967], [Mullins 1981][research_mullins_1981], [Teles 1989][research_teles_1989], [Peinan 1993][research_peinan_1993], [Shapiro and Bhat 1993][research_shapiro_bhat_1993], [Shapiro et al 1993][research_shapiro_1993], [Shapiro 1994][research_shapiro_1994], [Bettner 1995][research_bettner_1995]. At the equator that is a ground distance of

$$d = R\,\Delta\lambda = 6.371\times 10^6 \times 0.3937 = 2{,}509\ \text{km}$$

An arbitrary landing site lies somewhere between two adjacent tracks, so it is at most half that distance from the nearer one. **And those two tracks are consecutive in time.** The requirement to reach a chosen site on one pass or the next is therefore

$$y_\text{req} = \tfrac{1}{2} R\,\Delta\lambda = 1{,}254\ \text{km}$$

### The Answer

Inverting the crossrange relation on that requirement gives the lift-to-drag ratio a returning vehicle must have,

$$\frac{L}{D} = \sqrt{\frac{y_\text{req}/R}{\pi^2/48}} = \sqrt{\frac{0.1968}{0.2056}} = 0.978$$

Solving the same requirement against the exact integration instead, which cannot be done in closed form and has to be bisected, gives

$$\left(\frac{L}{D}\right)_\text{exact} = 1.018$$

**The correction moves the answer closer to unity rather than away from it**, which is a fortunate accident and not an argument for either number. Both are reported because the first is what the period could compute and the second is what is true.

**A lift-to-drag ratio near unity is not a round number chosen for convenience. It is the threshold at which a spacecraft stops being a passenger of its orbit and starts being able to come home when it wants to.** Below it the vehicle waits for the ground track to come back, which for a general orbit means a day or more and may mean never. At it, the longest wait is one orbit.

The requirement falls at higher latitudes, because the tracks crowd together as the meridians converge. The spacing carries a cosine, so the required ratio carries its square root,

$$y_\text{req}(\varphi) = \tfrac{1}{2} R\,\Delta\lambda\cos\varphi, \qquad \left(\frac{L}{D}\right)_\text{req}(\varphi) = \left(\frac{L}{D}\right)_\text{req}(0)\sqrt{\cos\varphi}$$

At 28.5 degrees that is 0.917, at Vandenberg's 34.7 degrees it is 0.887, and at 45 degrees it is 0.823. **The hardest case is the equator**, and a vehicle sized for the equator is sized for everywhere.

## What the Flight Record Says About a Disputed Number

The sources do not agree on this vehicle's hypersonic lift-to-drag ratio. One gives 1.0 and another gives 1.2 for the SV-5 shape.

As ratios those differ by twenty percent, which sounds tolerable. **They do not differ by twenty percent in the quantity this article is about**, because crossrange goes as the square,

$$\frac{y(1.2)}{y(1.0)} = \left(\frac{1.2}{1.0}\right)^2 = 1.44$$

**A twenty percent disagreement about the vehicle is a forty-four percent disagreement about what it can do**, and it cannot be waved away.

It can, however, be attacked, because two facts about the third flight are independently known and both are consequences of the same trajectory. The vehicle achieved 1,143 kilometres of crossrange, and it flew from Vandenberg Air Force Base to a recovery area near Kwajalein, a great-circle distance of

$$d = 2R\arcsin\sqrt{\sin^2\frac{\Delta\varphi}{2} + \cos\varphi_1\cos\varphi_2\sin^2\frac{\Delta\lambda}{2}} = 7{,}787\ \text{km}$$

The equilibrium glide gives a downrange to go with the crossrange, and it comes from the same two statements. Distance along the path accumulates at $\dot x = V$, and dividing by the deceleration gives

$$\frac{dx}{dV} = -\frac{V\,(L/D)\cos\phi}{g\left(1-u\right)}$$

Applying the same substitution as before, every factor of $V_c$ again cancels and the integrand becomes elementary,

$$dx = -\frac{R}{2}\,\frac{L}{D}\cos\phi\,\frac{du}{1-u}$$

which integrates directly to

$$x = \frac{L}{D}\cos\phi \;\frac{R}{2}\ln\frac{1}{1-u_e}$$

**Downrange is linear in the lift-to-drag ratio where crossrange is quadratic in it**, which is the reason the two together determine both unknowns. Banking to gain crossrange costs downrange as $\cos\phi$, so at the optimum the vehicle gives up

$$1 - \cos 45^\circ = 0.293$$

or twenty-nine percent of the distance it could have flown straight ahead.

**Two equations, two unknowns.** Writing $A$ and $B$ for the two geometric factors, the pair is $\;(L/D)\cos\phi = x/A\;$ and $\;(L/D)^2\sin\phi\cos\phi = y/B$, and dividing one by the square of the other gives the bank angle without needing the lift-to-drag ratio first,

$$\tan\phi = \frac{y/B}{(x/A)^2}, \qquad \frac{L}{D} = \frac{x/A}{\cos\phi}$$

**A first attempt at this check assumed a bank angle of 45 degrees and recovered 45.13, which looked like a confirmation and was nothing of the kind.** The assumed lift-to-drag ratio of 1.2 had been fed straight back in. The suspiciously clean agreement was the signal that the check was circular, and the fix was to stop assuming either quantity and solve for both.

The glide does not begin at the launch pad. The Atlas covers part of that great circle before entry interface, and how much is not published, so the honest treatment is to sweep it. The pair is solved twice, once with the closed form and once against the exact integration.

| Boost range before entry | Bank, closed form | Ratio, closed form | Bank, exact | Ratio, exact |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 km | 35.1 degrees | 1.035 | 37.0 degrees | 1.060 |
| 500 km | 38.8 degrees | 1.016 | 41.0 degrees | 1.050 |
| 1,000 km | 42.8 degrees | 1.006 | 45.5 degrees | 1.052 |
| 1,500 km | 47.2 degrees | 1.006 | 50.3 degrees | 1.070 |
| 2,000 km | 51.9 degrees | 1.019 | 55.5 degrees | 1.111 |
| 2,500 km | 56.8 degrees | 1.049 | 60.9 degrees | 1.183 |

Each solve carries its own bank angle, because the two models do not agree on what bank produces a given crossrange and pairing one model's ratio with the other's angle would be meaningless.

**The bank angle swings through twenty-four degrees and the lift-to-drag ratio moves very little.** Across the whole plausible range of the unknown the exact solution stays between 1.05 and 1.18. The flight record determines one of the two unknowns much better than the other, which is a more useful outcome than a single number would have been.

**The result sits between the two disputed figures and is nearer the lower one.** Flying the exact model at a ratio of 1.0 produces 1,043 kilometres against the 1,143 demonstrated, an under-prediction of 8.8 percent. Flying it at 1.2 produces 1,453 kilometres, an over-prediction of 27 percent. **Neither published figure reproduces the flight and the truth is in between**, somewhere near 1.05 to 1.10.

That is worth stating plainly because it is not the answer this analysis set out to find. The closed form alone appeared to vindicate the 1.0 figure to within one percent, and correcting the approximation took that vindication away and replaced it with a value that neither source gives.

**It does not settle the conflict and this article does not claim that it does.** Several readings survive. If 1,143 kilometres was the vehicle's maximum capability, the solution above is a measurement and both published figures are somewhat wrong. If it was a demanded value below capability, the solution is a lower bound and the 1.2 figure survives comfortably while 1.0 does not. There is also a reading in which both sources are right about different things, with 1.2 the maximum trimmed ratio the shape can reach and 1.0 a nominal or rounded value, since nothing obliges a vehicle to fly at its best lift-to-drag ratio.

**What can be said without qualification is narrower and firmer.** The vehicle's effective lift-to-drag ratio through the manoeuvring phase of the third flight was close to one, it was not close to zero, and a ballistic vehicle on the same trajectory would have landed 1,143 kilometres from where this one did.

### Thermal Protection

The equilibrium glide fixes the density at every speed, because the force balance demands it. Rearranging,

$$\rho = \frac{2\beta_L(1-u)}{V^2\cos\phi}, \qquad \beta_L = \frac{W}{S\,C_L}$$

so the heating history is a consequence of the trajectory and not a design choice. The companion quantity is the ballistic coefficient, which is the same construction on the drag side,

$$\beta = \frac{W}{S\,C_D}$$

and the two stand in a fixed ratio, which is a useful consistency check on the assumed coefficients,

$$\frac{\beta}{\beta_L} = \frac{C_L}{C_D} = \frac{L}{D}$$

With the derived reference area and an assumed hypersonic drag coefficient of unity,

$$\beta = \frac{3{,}977}{1.385 \times 1.0} = 2{,}870\ \text{N/m}^2 = 60\ \text{lb/ft}^2$$

and the lift parameter is 2,392 newtons per square metre. **That is a low ballistic coefficient, and low is what a research vehicle wants**, because it decelerates high up where the air is thin. The quantity is the single most compressed description of an entry vehicle there is, and the period literature treats it as such, in [Scherberg and Rubin 1953][research_scherberg_rubin_1953], [Schweppe 1964][research_schweppe_1964], [Chen 1965][research_chen_1965], [Crowe and Carlson 1970][research_crowe_carlson_1970], [Schoenenberger et al 2005][research_schoenenberger_2005].

Stagnation-point heat flux at a blunt nose is one of the most heavily worked correlations in the subject, and the accumulated body of it is [Eggers et al 1958][research_eggers_1958], [Goulard 1961][research_goulard_1961], [Kivel 1961][research_kivel_1961], [Scala 1961, THEORETICAL AND ANALYTICAL INVEST][research_scala_1961], [Wittliff and Wilson 1961][research_wittliff_wilson_1961], [Scala 1962][research_scala_1962], [Chow 1963][research_chow_1963], [Fay and Kemp 1963][research_fay_kemp_1963], [Rose and Stankevics 1963][research_rose_stankevics_1963], [Reilly 1964][research_reilly_1964], [Horton and Zeh 1966][research_horton_zeh_1966], [Tokuda and Yang 1966][research_tokuda_yang_1966], [Derienzo and Pallone 1967, Addendum Wonvective Stagnation-Po][research_derienzo_pallone_1967_2], [Derienzo and Pallone 1967, Convective stagnation-point heati][research_derienzo_pallone_1967], [Anderson 1968, Nongray radiative stagnation poin][research_anderson_1968_2], [Vidal 1974][research_vidal_1974], [Bade 1975][research_bade_1975], [Nomura 1983][research_nomura_1983], [Gupta et al 1992][research_gupta_1992]. The form used here is that of Sutton and Graves,

$$\dot q_s = k\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{R_n}}\,V^3, \qquad k = 1.7415\times 10^{-4}$$

in metric units for air, with the density in kilogrammes per cubic metre, the nose radius in metres and the speed in metres per second. Substituting the glide density collapses the speed dependence. Since $\rho \propto (1-u)/V^2$, the square root carries $\sqrt{(1-u)/u}$ and the cube carries $u^{3/2}$, so

$$\dot q_s \propto \sqrt{\frac{1-u}{u}}\;u^{3/2} = u\sqrt{1-u}$$

Maximising is easiest on the square, whose derivative is a quadratic,

$$\frac{d}{du}\left[u^2(1-u)\right] = 2u - 3u^2 = 0 \quad \Longrightarrow \quad u = \tfrac{2}{3}$$

so the worst heating happens at

$$u = \tfrac{2}{3}, \qquad V = 0.8165\,V_c = 6{,}454\ \text{m/s}$$

**Peak heating is at eighty-two percent of circular speed regardless of the vehicle**, because every property of it cancelled on the way to that quadratic.

The density there follows from the glide balance,

$$\rho = \frac{2 \times 2{,}392 \times \tfrac{1}{3}}{6{,}454^2 \times \cos 45^\circ} = 5.41\times 10^{-5}\ \text{kg/m}^3$$

and with an assumed nose radius of 0.1 metres the peak is

$$\dot q_{s,\text{max}} = 1.7415\times 10^{-4}\sqrt{\frac{5.41\times 10^{-5}}{0.1}}\,\left(6{,}454\right)^3 = 109\ \text{W/cm}^2$$

which is modest by the standards of a ballistic capsule and sustained for far longer.

**That correlation accounts for convection and not for radiation, and the omission is deliberate rather than careless.** Shock-layer radiation becomes a significant part of the heat load only above roughly ten kilometres per second, which is the regime of a lunar or interplanetary return rather than of an orbital one. PRIME entered at 7.7, so the radiative term is small and the convective correlation carries the analysis. **At Apollo's entry speed it would not**, and the literature of that regime is [Christopher O Johnston][research_christopher_o_johnston], [Yoshikawa and Wick 1961][research_yoshikawa_wick_1961], [Koh 1962][research_koh_1962], [Nerem and Stickford 1964][research_nerem_stickford_1964], [Cooper 1966, Equilibrium air radiation from sh][research_cooper_1966], [Cooper 1966, High-resolution experimental spec][research_cooper_1966_2], [Analytical, experimental, and developmental 1967][research_analytical_experimental_1967], [Bloor 1967][research_bloor_1967], [Engel and Spradley 1968][research_engel_spradley_1968], [Liu 1968][research_liu_1968], [Olstad 1969][research_olstad_1969], [Biberman et al 1972][research_biberman_1972], [Golovachev 1972][research_golovachev_1972], [Page and Woodward 1972][research_page_woodward_1972], [Edquist 1974][research_edquist_1974], [Sutton 1976][research_sutton_1976], [Winston and Krueger 1977][research_winston_krueger_1977], [Golovachev 1979, Estimates of nonequilibrium radia][research_golovachev_1979], [Nelson 1980][research_nelson_1980], [Nelson 1983][research_nelson_1983].

Inverting an exponential atmosphere of scale height 7,160 metres,

$$h = -H\ln\frac{\rho}{\rho_0} = -7{,}160\ln\left(4.42\times 10^{-5}\right) = 71.8\ \text{km}$$

or about 236,000 feet. **The vehicle takes its worst heating some forty-five kilometres above where an airliner cruises and never comes near the speeds involved again.** Integrating the flux over the glide, using $dt = -(L/D)\cos\phi\,dV/(g(1-u))$, gives a closed form,

$$Q = k\,\frac{L}{D}\sqrt{\cos\phi}\sqrt{\frac{2\beta_L}{R_n}}\frac{V_c^3}{g}\cdot\frac{\arcsin a - a\sqrt{1-a^2}}{2}$$

with $a = \sqrt{u_e}$, evaluating to 107 kilojoules per square centimetre. The same integral gives the duration,

$$t = \frac{L}{D}\cos\phi\,\frac{V_c}{g}\,\text{artanh}\,\sqrt{u_e} = 1{,}452\ \text{s}$$

**Twenty-four minutes.** That is the price of using the lift. A ballistic capsule is through the heating in two or three.

Now a cross-check that was not designed in. The heat shield thickness is published, and the heat load has just been computed, so the effective heat of ablation is determined,

$$H_\text{eff} = \frac{Q}{\rho_\text{abl}\,t_\text{abl}}$$

At the thick end of the published range, 2.75 inches at a representative charring-ablator density of 550 kilograms per cubic metre, the areal mass and the implied heat of ablation are

$$\rho_\text{abl}t_\text{abl} = 550 \times 0.0699 = 38.4\ \text{kg/m}^2, \qquad H_\text{eff} = \frac{1.068\times 10^9}{38.4} = 27.8\ \text{MJ/kg}$$

**That is squarely a real material property for a charring ablator.** At the thin end of the range, 0.75 inches, it gives 102 megajoules per kilogram, which no ablator achieves. **The two published thicknesses therefore bracket the heating distribution and not the uncertainty in it**, with the thick end at the stagnation region and the thin end on surfaces that never see anything like the stagnation load. The arithmetic identifies which end is which without being told.

### Control

The vehicle's entire guidance task was to hold a bank angle. **That is the whole control law, and the derivation above is the reason.** Attitude was held by nitrogen reaction jets at high altitude and by flaps once the dynamic pressure was sufficient, in [Wei Wu, AN AXIS SEQUENTIAL REACTION CONTR][research_wei_wu], [Wei Wu, An Axis Sequential Reaction Contr][research_wei_wu_2], [Patapoff 1961][research_patapoff_1961], [Tinling 1962][research_tinling_1962], [Lopez and Ratcliff 1964][research_lopez_ratcliff_1964], [Huston 1966][research_huston_1966], [Kanning 1966][research_kanning_1966], [Fine 1967][research_fine_1967], [Greer and Griep 1967][research_greer_griep_1967], [Seltzer 1968][research_seltzer_1968], [Christensen and Ko 1969][research_christensen_ko_1969], [Hill Jr. and Smith 1969][research_hill_jr_smith_1969], [Seltzer 1969][research_seltzer_1969], [Hooker et al 1970][research_hooker_1970], [Moran and Dishman 1970][research_moran_dishman_1970], [Seltzer 1970, Nonlinear analysis of a launch ve][research_seltzer_1970_2], [Sorensen 1971][research_sorensen_1971], [Bernussou 1973][research_bernussou_1973], [Dziubala et al 1973][research_dziubala_1973], [Blevins and Hohmann 1975][research_blevins_hohmann_1975], [Fester et al 1975][research_fester_1975], [Monopropellant engine investigation for 1975][research_monopropellant_engine_1975], [Scott and Rubbo 1975][research_scott_rubbo_1975], [Stickler and Alfriend 1976][research_stickler_alfriend_1976], [Colburn and White 1977][research_colburn_white_1977], [Garg 1979][research_garg_1979], [Scarboro 1982][research_scarboro_1982], [Bittner et al 1983][research_bittner_1983], [Reusable Reentry Satellite RRS 1991, Reusable Reentry Satellite RRS sy][research_reusable_reentry_1991], [Bailey et al 2008][research_bailey_2008]. ### Deceleration, and Why the Glide Is Gentle

The same balance that sets the density sets the deceleration. In equilibrium glide it is

$$\frac{a}{g} = \frac{1-u}{(L/D)\cos\phi}$$

which is smallest at the beginning and greatest at the end. At entry, where $u = 0.944$, it is

$$\frac{a}{g} = \frac{1 - 0.944}{1.2\cos 45^\circ} = 0.066$$

so the vehicle barely feels the atmosphere at first, and by the end of the glide it has risen only to

$$\frac{a}{g} = \frac{1}{1.2\cos 45^\circ} = 1.18$$

**The entire lifting entry is gentler than standing up.** The same entry flown ballistically, at the reported flight path angle of 6 degrees, gives the Allen and Eggers result

$$a_\text{max} = \frac{V_e^2\sin\gamma_e}{2eH} = \frac{\left(7{,}681\right)^2\sin 6^\circ}{2e \times 7{,}160} = 158\ \text{m/s}^2 = 16.2\ g$$

where $H$ is the atmospheric scale height. **The unlifted case is not a strawman**, since it is how every warhead and every early capsule came back, and it has a large literature of its own against which the lifting case is the departure, in [John DiNonno and F. McNeil Cheatwood][research_john_dinonno_f_mcneil_cheatwood], [R J Bodkin et al][research_r_j_bodkin], [Stephen Hughes et al][research_stephen_hughes], [Stephen J Hughes et al, Low-Earth Orbit Flight Test of an][research_stephen_j_hughes], [Stephen J Hughes et al, Low-Earth Orbit Flight Test of an][research_stephen_j_hughes_2], [MacCarthy 1954][research_maccarthy_1954], [Fasola et al 1955][research_fasola_1955], [Laitone 1959][research_laitone_1959], [Phillips and Cohen 1959][research_phillips_cohen_1959], [Foster 1960][research_foster_1960], [Hamaker 1960][research_hamaker_1960], [Tomcsak 1960][research_tomcsak_1960], [Diaconis et al 1961][research_diaconis_1961], [Fink 1961][research_fink_1961], [Heinrich 1961][research_heinrich_1961], [Jaskowski 1961][research_jaskowski_1961], [Luidens 1961][research_luidens_1961], [Greensite 1962][research_greensite_1962], [Gunder 1962][research_gunder_1962], [Jaskowski 1962][research_jaskowski_1962], [Lenihan 1962][research_lenihan_1962], [Loh 1962, Ballistic Re-Entry at Small Angle][research_loh_1962], [Loh 1962, On Exact Numerical and Approximat][research_loh_1962_2], [Pollak 1962][research_pollak_1962], [Southworth 1962][research_southworth_1962], [Bequaert and Chin 1963][research_bequaert_chin_1963], [Ham 1963][research_ham_1963], [Samuel 1963][research_samuel_1963], [Kumagai 1964][research_kumagai_1964], [Lykoudis 1964][research_lykoudis_1964], [Deitering and Hilliard 1965][research_deitering_hilliard_1965], [I. The ratio is

$$\frac{16.2}{1.18} = 13.7$$

**A factor of nearly fourteen.** The lift is not only buying crossrange. It is buying a survivable deceleration, and for the piloted vehicles that this programme was clearing the way for, that was the more immediately valuable of the two.

### Recovery, Which Is a Data Return System

A vehicle that measures something and then sinks has measured nothing that cannot be telemetered. **The recovery system is therefore instrumentation**, and it is the part of PRIME that failed twice.

The sequence was a ballute at about Mach 2, then a 47-foot parachute, then mid-air retrieval by a JC-130B using the technique already in service for [returning film capsules][ref_corona].

The ballute deploys near 99,850 feet, or 30.4 kilometres, where the speed of sound follows from the local temperature,

$$a = \sqrt{\gamma R_\text{air} T} = \sqrt{1.4 \times 287 \times 226.5} = 302\ \text{m/s}$$

so Mach 2 is 603 metres per second and the fabric has to be opened against a load that the supersonic-decelerator literature exists to predict, in [Alejandro Pensado et al, Modeling and Simulation of the Dr][research_alejandro_pensado_2], [Alejandro R Pensado et al][research_alejandro_r_pensado], [Evan Roelke and Justin Green][research_evan_roelke_justin_green], [Evan Roelke et al, Advanced Supersonic Parachute Inf][research_evan_roelke], [Evan Roelke et al, Advanced Supersonic Parachute Inf][research_evan_roelke_2], [F Cadieux and M Barad][research_f_cadieux_m_barad], [Francois Cadieux and Michael F Barad, Fluid-Structure Interaction Simul][research_francois_cadieux_michael_f_barad_2], [Francois Cadieux and Michael F Barad, Supersonic Parachute Inflation Si][research_francois_cadieux_michael_f_barad], [Francois Cadieux et al][research_francois_cadieux], [Jonathan Boustani et al][research_jonathan_boustani], [Sims 1963][research_sims_1963], [Heinrich 1966, Aerodynamics of the supersonic gu][research_heinrich_1966_2], [Mcewan 1970][research_mcewan_1970], [Poole 1973, Effect of suspension-line viscous][research_poole_1973], [Poole 1973, Effect of suspension-line viscous][research_poole_1973_2], [Brinkman 1992][research_brinkman_1992], [Gnoffo and Anderson 2002][research_gnoffo_anderson_2002], [Cruz et al 2003][research_cruz_2003], [Miller et al 2003][research_miller_2003],

$$q = \tfrac{1}{2}\rho V^2 = \tfrac{1}{2} \times 0.0184 \times 603^2 = 3{,}350\ \text{Pa} = 70\ \text{lb/ft}^2$$

**That is a mild load by supersonic-decelerator standards**, which is the point of deploying that high and that late.

The recovery parachute's area follows from its diameter,

$$S_p = \frac{\pi D^2}{4} = \frac{\pi \times 14.33^2}{4} = 161\ \text{m}^2$$

and under it the vehicle descends at

$$V_d = \sqrt{\frac{2W}{\rho\,C_D S_p}} = \sqrt{\frac{2 \times 3{,}977}{1.225 \times 0.75 \times 161}} = 7.33\ \text{m/s}$$

or 24 feet per second, which is what a mid-air catch needs. The literature of that technique is [Braun 1953][research_braun_1953], [Downing et al 1956][research_downing_1956], [Gillette 1963][research_gillette_1963], [French 1965][research_french_1965], [French 1966][research_french_1966], [Jamison 1966][research_jamison_1966], [Eckstrom and Preisser 1967][research_eckstrom_preisser_1967], [Jamison 1967][research_jamison_1967], [Neustadt et al 1967][research_neustadt_1967], [Preisser et al 1967][research_preisser_1967], [Cassanto et al 1968][research_cassanto_1968], [Brearley 1969][research_brearley_1969], [Chakoian 1969][research_chakoian_1969], [Goodrick 1969][research_goodrick_1969], [Pepper and Cronin 1969][research_pepper_cronin_1969], [Eckstrom 1970][research_eckstrom_1970], [Michal et al 1970][research_michal_1970], [Wolf 1971][research_wolf_1971], [Pearson 1972][research_pearson_1972], [Pepper and Biesterveld 1973][research_pepper_biesterveld_1973], [Whitlock et al 1973][research_whitlock_1973], [Wei and Pearson 1974][research_wei_pearson_1974], [Wyatt 1974][research_wyatt_1974], [Fu and Koch 1977][research_fu_koch_1977], [Pepper 1980][research_pepper_1980], [Roberts 1980][research_roberts_1980], [Schatzle and Curry 1980][research_schatzle_curry_1980], [Pepper and Collins 1982][research_pepper_collins_1982], [High Drag Rotating Parachute Developed and Tested 1985][research_high_drag_1985], [Knacke 1985][research_knacke_1985], [Yavuz 1985][research_yavuz_1985], [Braisted 1989][research_braisted_1989], [Sengupta et al 2009][research_sengupta_2009].

## The Flight Test Record

Three vehicles flew. A fourth was built and not launched.

| Flight | Date | Crossrange demanded | Outcome |
|---|---|---|---|
| PRIME 1 | 21 December 1966 | zero | Parachute did not fully deploy. Vehicle lost in the Pacific. |
| PRIME 2 | 5 March 1967 | 1,053 km | Parachute reefing cutters failed. Vehicle lost. |
| PRIME 3 | 19 April 1967 | 1,143 km | All systems nominal. Recovered in mid-air. |

**The programme's three flights were a failure, a failure and a success, and it is important to be clear about what failed.** In neither of the first two cases did the vehicle fail to fly the entry. Both flew their profiles and both telemetered. What failed was the recovery chain, twice, in two different components. The third flight demonstrated the largest crossrange of the three and was the one that came back.

The vehicle that flew PRIME 3 is at the [National Museum of the United States Air Force][ref_nmusaf_sv5d].

## Comparison With Ground Prediction

| Quantity | Model | Flight |
|---|---|---|
| Crossrange at a lift-to-drag ratio of 1.0, closed form | 1,133 km | 1,143 km |
| Crossrange at a lift-to-drag ratio of 1.0, exact | 1,043 km | 1,143 km |
| Crossrange at a lift-to-drag ratio of 1.2, exact | 1,453 km | 1,143 km |
| Downrange at the solved bank angle | 7,787 km by construction | Vandenberg to Kwajalein |
| Peak deceleration | 1.18 g | not published in the accessible record |
| Glide duration | 24.2 min | consistent with the reported profile |

**The closed form is good to one percent at the lower of the two disputed lift-to-drag ratios, and that agreement is an accident of two errors cancelling.** The small-angle step over-predicts by eight percent and the assumed ratio is low by about the same, so the product comes out right for the wrong reason. Removing the approximation removes the coincidence and leaves the flight sitting between the two published figures.

**That is the most instructive thing here about method rather than about the vehicle.** A model that agrees with a measurement to one percent is not thereby correct, and the way to find out is to improve the model and see whether the agreement survives. Here it did not.

The comparison is weaker than it looks in one respect that must be said. PRIME entered on a depressed suborbital trajectory at a flight path angle near 6 degrees, which is considerably steeper than the shallow entry the equilibrium glide assumes. The model describes the mission being simulated more faithfully than the trajectory actually flown.

## What the Data Changed

PRIME fed the X-24A directly. The same shape, at larger scale and with a pilot, was flown at Edwards from 1969, and the aerodynamic data from the unmanned entries reduced the risk of the piloted programme in the regime where no piloted vehicle had been. The vehicles that followed, and the manned entry programmes this one was clearing ground for, are [Driscoll et al 1966][research_driscoll_1966], [George E Mueller et al 1967][research_george_e_mueller_1967], [Minott et al 1976][research_minott_1976], [Duggan 1977][research_duggan_1977], [Space Shuttle maiden 1979][research_space_shuttle_1979], [Waldrop 1980][research_waldrop_1980], [McCammon 1982][research_mccammon_1982], [Mckenna et al 1982][research_mckenna_1982], [Grooms et al 1984][research_grooms_1984], [Bellows et al 1985][research_bellows_1985], [Joosten 1985][research_joosten_1985], [Logsdon 1986][research_logsdon_1986], [Space shuttle log 1986][research_space_shuttle_1986], [Zahn and Murad 1986][research_zahn_murad_1986], [Shuttle gets safety reporting system 1987][research_shuttle_gets_1987], [Space shuttle experiments 1987, Space shuttle experiments][research_space_shuttle_1987], [Space shuttle flight rates 1987, Space shuttle flight rates and ut][research_space_shuttle_1987_2], [Space shuttle setback 1987, Space shuttle setback][research_space_shuttle_1987_3], [Weinberg 1987][research_weinberg_1987], [Abdul-Aziz et al 1989][research_abdul_aziz_1989], [Brookfield et al 1989][research_brookfield_1989], [Whitesides et al 1990][research_whitesides_1990], [Design of a workstand for overhead operations: Case of the space shuttle heat-tile replacement 1991][research_design_of_1991], [Ware et al 1991][research_ware_1991], [Cruz and Ware 1992][research_cruz_ware_1992], [Sawko and Goldstein 1992][research_sawko_goldstein_1992], [Iliff and Shafer 1993][research_iliff_shafer_1993], [Pielke 1993][research_pielke_1993], [Ware and Cruz 1993][research_ware_cruz_1993], [Pielke 1994][research_pielke_1994], [Chaudhary et al 2001][research_chaudhary_2001], [Straube et al 2002][research_straube_2002], [McCluskey 2004][research_mccluskey_2004], [Krantz and Oswald 2005][research_krantz_oswald_2005].

**The larger consequence is the Space Shuttle's crossrange requirement, and there is an arithmetic connection worth showing.** The requirement, the entry it drove, and the guidance built to fly it are documented at length, in [Adams 1971][research_adams_1971], [Dejarnette and Jones 1971][research_dejarnette_jones_1971], [Deyst et al 1971, Optimal lateral guidance for low][research_deyst_1971], [Deyst et al 1971, Optimal lateral guidance for low][research_deyst_1971_2], [Deyst et al 1972][research_deyst_1972], [Mcglathery 1973][research_mcglathery_1973], [Space shuttle guidance, navigation 1973][research_space_shuttle_1973], [Stolarski et al 1974][research_stolarski_1974], [Kriegsman and Tao 1975][research_kriegsman_tao_1975], [Ramnath and Sinha 1975][research_ramnath_sinha_1975], [Jeffs 1979][research_jeffs_1979], [Emero 1980][research_emero_1980], [Park and Rakich 1980][research_park_rakich_1980], [Rowell et al 1980][research_rowell_1980], [Iliff et al 1981][research_iliff_1981], [Compton et al 1982][research_compton_1982], [Fraser 1983][research_fraser_1983], [Kanipe 1983][research_kanipe_1983], [Romere and Young 1983][research_romere_young_1983], [Romere et al 1983][research_romere_1983], [Bayle 1984][research_bayle_1984], [Cox and Hattis 1987][research_cox_hattis_1987], [Campbell et al 1989][research_campbell_1989], [Rosello 1995][research_rosello_1995], [Restrepo and Valasek 2006][research_restrepo_valasek_2006], [Campbell 2007][research_campbell_2007], [Wood and Alter 2008][research_wood_alter_2008], [Wood et al 2008][research_wood_2008]. The Shuttle was required to achieve 1,100 nautical miles of crossrange, a figure that came from the Air Force and was driven by a once-around polar mission returning to its launch site. The launch site moves with the Earth, so in one orbit it displaces by the full track spacing at its own latitude rather than half of it,

$$d_\text{VAFB} = R\,\Delta\lambda\cos\varphi = 6.371\times 10^6 \times 0.3937 \times \cos 34.74^\circ = 2{,}061\ \text{km}$$

against a stated requirement of 1,100 nautical miles, which is 2,037 kilometres. The agreement is

$$\frac{2{,}061 - 2{,}037}{2{,}037} = 1.2\ \text{percent}$$

**and nothing here was fitted to anything.** The requirement that shaped the Space Shuttle's wing, and through it a great deal of the vehicle, is the distance Vandenberg travels while a spacecraft goes round once.

**That requirement also explains why the Shuttle has wings and PRIME did not.** Putting 1,100 nautical miles through the same relation that gave 0.978 for the general case,

$$\frac{L}{D} = \sqrt{\frac{2{,}037/6{,}371}{0.2056}} = 1.25$$

which is well above what a pure lifting body of the period could reach and is roughly what a delta wing at hypersonic incidence does reach. **The once-around requirement bought the Shuttle its wing.**

PRIME demonstrated a fraction

$$\frac{1{,}143}{2{,}037} = 0.56$$

of that figure with a vehicle weighing 894 pounds.

The programme is also a data point about unmanned research vehicles, which the designation section below turns out to bear on directly.

## A Designation That Was Never Requested

The vehicle in the museum is labelled X-23A. The encyclopaedias call it the X-23A. **The nomenclature records, as reported by the [standard reference on United States military aircraft designations][ref_designation_missing], show that the designation was never assigned, and further that it was never requested.**

The sequence, which is stranger than a simple omission, runs as follows.

On **16 November 1965** a request was submitted for the designation X-23A. It was submitted for the **SV-5P**, the piloted low-speed vehicle, and the vehicle description accompanying the request clearly described a manned lifting-body aircraft flying from Mach 2 to landing.

On **15 December 1965** the request was disapproved, **on the ground that the subject aircraft was unmanned**. At that date the designation system applied to manned aircraft only, so the reason was a valid one in general. It was simply not true of this aircraft, and the paperwork accompanying the request said so.

In **late 1966** the offices responsible reconsidered the whole question. A proposal to create an entirely new designation category for gliding re-entry vehicles was raised and abandoned. The decision instead was to request X-23A for the unmanned SV-5D and X-24A for the piloted SV-5P. **X-24A was requested and approved. No request for X-23A appears ever to have been sent.**

The reason is not recorded. The plausible reading, offered as a reading and not as a finding, is that having been told a year earlier that unmanned aircraft need no designation, nobody saw the point of asking.

**Three things about this are worth separating.**

**First, the designation is in universal use and is not official.** This article uses it, as its title does, because refusing to would make the vehicle harder to find without making it more accurately described.

**Second, the disapproval was wrong on its facts.** The 1965 refusal turned on the vehicle being unmanned, and the vehicle in that request was the piloted one. The manned vehicle was refused a number for being unmanned, and a year later received a different number for being manned.

**Third, and most useful to this series, the system briefly considered growing a new limb and did not.** A separate designation category for gliding re-entry vehicles was proposed and dropped. **The X-series absorbed the problem instead, informally and without paperwork**, which is a small piece of evidence about how the designation system actually accommodated things it was not built for. The series' closing article argues that the designation system is not a counter. This is one of the cases that argument rests on, and it should be read alongside the reserved-but-never-allocated X-39 and the refused X-52.

**This article states the conflict and does not resolve it.** The records say the number was never assigned. The world says the vehicle is the X-23A. Both statements are true about different things, and collapsing them would destroy the evidence.

## The Contemporary Literature

The problem PRIME was built for did not go away. **What happened instead is stranger and is the organising fact of this survey. The capability was demonstrated, it was adopted, it was then given up by the very class of vehicle it had been built for, and it was adopted again by a different class of vehicle for the opposite reason.**

### The Vehicles It Was Built For Gave the Capability Up

This article's own relation is still the quickest way to see what that costs. A blunt capsule trims at a lift-to-drag ratio near 0.3, and the crossrange that buys is

$$y = \pi^2/48 \times 0.3^2 \times 6.371\times 10^6 = 118\ \text{km}$$

or 117 kilometres by the exact integration, the two being close here because at so small a lift-to-drag ratio the heading barely turns and the small-angle step costs almost nothing. Set against the once-per-orbit requirement of 1,254 kilometres derived earlier, that is

$$\frac{117}{1{,}254} = 0.09$$

**Nine percent.** A modern crewed spacecraft has, by deliberate choice, about one eleventh of the crossrange that would let it come home when it wants to.

| Vehicle | Lift-to-drag ratio | Crossrange, exact |
|---|---|---|
| A ballistic capsule with no lift | 0 | 0 km |
| Mercury and Vostok | 0.05 | 3 km |
| Apollo, Orion and Soyuz | 0.30 | 117 km |
| PRIME and the SV-5 family | 1.00 | 1,214 km |
| The Space Shuttle orbiter | 1.10 | 1,447 km |
| The X-24A | 1.40 | 2,233 km |

**The regression is not a failure of engineering. It is the requirement disappearing.** The once-around polar mission that generated the Shuttle's 1,100 nautical miles was an Air Force requirement, and when it lapsed nothing else needed that number. An ocean landing needs no runway, a parachute weighs less than a wing, and a capsule's heat shield is a simpler object than an orbiter's. **The Shuttle's wing was the last time crossrange drove a crewed vehicle's shape**, and PRIME is part of the reason that wing could be drawn with confidence.

### Crossrange Became a Weapon's Virtue

The capability did not disappear. It moved, in [Chen et al 2015, Integrated Guidance and Control M][research_chen_2015], [Ding et al 2015][research_ding_2015], [Liu and Lu 2015][research_liu_lu_2015], [Schneider thermodynamic-dynamic m][research_de_moura_ribeiro_2026_2].

**A hypersonic glide vehicle wants lateral range for exactly the reason a spacecraft did not.** A returning spacecraft wanted to reach a chosen site. A glide vehicle wants a trajectory that cannot be extrapolated, because an interceptor must be committed early and a manoeuvring target invalidates the prediction it was committed against. The literature reflects that inversion precisely, being largely about estimating and tracking a manoeuvring entry body, where the period literature was about designing one.

**The relation is the same and the motive is not, and it is worth noticing that the equation cannot tell the difference.** The derivation above contains nothing that distinguishes a landing site from an evasive manoeuvre.

There is a limit worth stating, and it constrains the treatment given here and not the literature. A slender glide vehicle may reach a lift-to-drag ratio of 2.5, and the closed form would offer 8,187 kilometres for it. **That number should not be believed.** At such a ratio the closed form over-predicts by 41 percent, the accumulated heading change passes 400 degrees, and even the exact constant-bank integration is describing a trajectory nobody would fly. **The relation that describes PRIME does not describe its successors**, and saying so is more useful than extrapolating it past its own assumptions.

The crossrange relation derived above is a closed form obtained by forcing the trajectory to be an equilibrium glide at constant bank. That was necessary in 1964 because the alternative was unaffordable. The modern equivalent solves the trajectory numerically, onboard, in real time, and increasingly by convex optimisation whose convergence can be guaranteed rather than hoped for. **It arrives at very nearly the same answer, because the physics did not move.**

**The closed form's role has changed rather than ended.** It is no longer how a vehicle is sized. It is how the answer from the optimiser is checked, which is exactly the use made of it above.

### The Ablator Is Computed Rather Than Tested

**The 1960s route to a heat shield thickness was arc-jet testing and margin.** The modern route computes the material response, in [Arnold et al 2013][research_arnold_2013], [Li et al 2015, A nonlinear pyrolysis layer model][research_li_2015_8], [Milos et al 2015][research_milos_2015], [Chen 2016, Thermal Ablation Modeling for Sil][research_chen_2016], [Grigoriu 2016][research_grigoriu_2016], [Kumar and Mahulikar 2016, Reconstruction of aero-thermal he][research_kumar_mahulikar_2016_2], [Brune et al 2017][research_brune_2017], [Harpale et al 2018][research_harpale_2018], [Meurisse et al 2018][research_meurisse_2018], [Cinquegrana et al 2019][research_cinquegrana_2019], [Gulli and Maddalena 2019][research_gulli_maddalena_2019], [Rivier et al 2019][research_rivier_2019], [Wang et al 2019, Assessment of a one-dimensional f][research_wang_2019_5], [Shilav and Khosid 2020][research_shilav_khosid_2020], [Pratibha Raghunandan et al 2021][research_pratibha_raghunandan_2021], [Hasan 2022][research_hasan_2022], [He et al 2022][research_he_2022], [Price et al 2022][research_price_2022], [Chinnaraj et al 2023][research_chinnaraj_2023], [He et al 2023][research_he_2023], [Lai and Leu 2023, Loosely coupled analysis strategy][research_lai_leu_2023], [Lai and Leu 2023, Two Coupled Analysis Strategies f][research_lai_leu_2023_2], [Scandelli et al 2023][research_scandelli_2023], [Chen and Gökçen 2024][research_chen_gokcen_2024], [Gholami Haghighi Fard and Hostikka 2024][research_gholami_haghighi_fard_hostikka_2024], [Yang et al 2024, ‘Temperature jump’ mechanism of s][research_yang_2024_2], [Price et al 2025][research_price_2025], [Wang et al 2025, Arc Jet Testing and Modeling Stud][research_wang_2025_3], [Cutrone et al 2026][research_cutrone_2026], [Zibitsker and Martin 2026][research_zibitsker_martin_2026].

That is the largest single practical change in the subject. The cross-check performed earlier in this article, inverting a published thickness to recover an effective heat of ablation of 27.8 megajoules per kilogram, is the crude version of what a material response code now does properly, with pyrolysis chemistry, in-depth conduction and a quantified uncertainty on the answer. **Arc-jet testing did not go away. It became validation instead of design.**

**What is flying now is closer to PRIME than to the X-24A**, in the sense that it is mostly uncrewed and mostly automatic. Alongside them sits a genuinely new idea the 1960s did not have, which is the deployable or inflatable aeroshell that packs small and opens to a large low-ballistic-coefficient decelerator. **That attacks the problem from the opposite end.** PRIME lowered its ballistic coefficient to 60 pounds per square foot by being light. A deployable aeroshell lowers it by growing.

Mid-air retrieval by helicopter has since been demonstrated on a returning orbital booster, and the supersonic parachute work that Mars entry demanded has produced a decelerator literature far deeper than anything available in 1967. **The technique outlived the aerodynamics**, which is not the outcome anyone would have predicted from a programme that lost two vehicles to reefing cutters and a parachute that did not fully open.

### The Footprint Became a Safety Calculation

Here the inversion is at its sharpest, in [García-Pelayo et al 2016][research_garcia_pelayo_2016], [Falsone and Prandini 2017][research_falsone_prandini_2017], [Kim et al 2018][research_kim_2018], [Lee et al 2018, Effective Computational Approach][research_lee_2018_2], [Track 2 Debris Removal 2019][research_track_2_2019], [Annaloro et al 2020][research_annaloro_2020], [Braun et al 2020, DRAMA 3.0 - Upgrade of ESA’s debr][research_braun_2020_2], [Holubek 2020][research_holubek_2020], [Miao and Liu 2020][research_miao_liu_2020], [Ye et al 2020][research_ye_2020], [Yoakum and Cerreta 2020][research_yoakum_cerreta_2020], [Jorgensen and Sharf 2021][research_jorgensen_sharf_2021], [Santos and Sampaio 2021][research_santos_sampaio_2021], [kanahara 2022][research_kanahara_2022], [Canoy and Bettinger 2023][research_canoy_bettinger_2023], [Bohman et al 2024][research_bohman_2024], [Liqiu et al 2024][research_liqiu_2024], [Reddy Adavelli 2024][research_reddy_adavelli_2024], [Yang et al 2024, Design of Low-Cost Simulation Spa][research_yang_2024_3], [Amjadian et al 2025][research_amjadian_2025], [Chen et al 2025, Enhanced covariance propagation f][research_chen_2025_6], [Chen et al 2025, Stochastic Modeling of Nonlinear][research_chen_2025_7], [Jain and Hastings 2025][research_jain_hastings_2025], [Reid and Bettinger 2025][research_reid_bettinger_2025], [Ruffini et al 2025][research_ruffini_2025], [Navaz and Ntantis 2026][research_navaz_ntantis_2026], [Park et al 2026][research_park_2026], [Yuricich 2026][research_yuricich_2026].

**PRIME wanted a large footprint. Modern practice wants a small and precisely predicted one.** The reachable area treated above as a capability is, for a spent stage or a decaying satellite, a hazard to be bounded, and the same trajectory mathematics now serves debris casualty risk, controlled disposal and range safety. **A quantity computed to show what a vehicle could reach is now computed to prove what it cannot.**

The equilibrium glide with a different gravitational parameter and a different atmosphere is what every planetary entry and every aerocapture study solves. Mars is where the modern work concentrates, because it has enough atmosphere to matter and not enough to make entry easy, and because the crossrange question there is the same question PRIME asked, being whether a vehicle can reach a chosen site rather than wherever the ballistic track puts it.

### What the Survey Shows

**The arithmetic has not moved at all.** Crossrange still goes as the square of the lift-to-drag ratio, the optimal bank angle is still 45 degrees, the footprint still goes as the cube, and the once-per-orbit requirement is still about a lift-to-drag ratio of one. Nothing in the modern literature disputes any of that, and most of it does not restate it because it is assumed.

**What moved twice is the requirement.** It went from the Air Force wanting a spacecraft that could come home on the next pass, to nobody wanting that and crewed vehicles giving up ninety-one percent of the capability, to a different community wanting lateral range specifically because it defeats prediction.

**And the thing PRIME was worst at turned out to be the thing that lasted.** The programme lost two of three vehicles to the recovery system. Mid-air retrieval is now a demonstrated commercial technique, and the aerodynamic configuration it was carrying is not flown by anyone.

## Where the Framing Breaks Down

**Treating PRIME through crossrange alone gets the programme wrong in at least three ways.**

**The vehicle was not only a manoeuvring experiment.** It carried a heat shield the programme wanted measured, and the ASSET half of START had already been devoted to exactly that. Heating and manoeuvring were both objectives and the article's keystone selects one. The wider class of vehicles it belongs to, most of which never manoeuvred at all, is [Derbidge and Dahm 1976][research_derbidge_dahm_1976], [Developing priority entry control and vehicle control strategies at freeway ramps 1976][research_developing_priority_1976], [Marinescu 1976][research_marinescu_1976], [Nikolaev et al 1976][research_nikolaev_1976], [Pande 1976][research_pande_1976], [Park 1976][research_park_1976], [Rubinstein 1976][research_rubinstein_1976], [Seiff and Kirk 1976][research_seiff_kirk_1976], [Ikawa 1977][research_ikawa_1977], [Johnson and Joseph A. 1977][research_johnson_joseph_a_1977], [Likins 1977][research_likins_1977], [Lyons 1977][research_lyons_1977], [Phillips et al 1977][research_phillips_1977], [Powell 1977][research_powell_1977], [Alexander and T. **The equilibrium glide is not what it flew.** The model assumes a shallow entry from orbit. PRIME was lobbed on a depressed trajectory at a much steeper flight path angle, and it reached the crossrange it did under conditions the model describes only approximately. That the numbers agree to one percent at the lower disputed lift-to-drag ratio is encouraging and is not proof.

**The reference area is derived, not published.** Everything in the thermal section inherits that. The crossrange result does not, which is why it is the keystone and the heating is not.

There is also a fourth, about the article's own framing. **A vehicle whose recovery system failed twice in three flights is a programme about recovery at least as much as about entry**, and it gets two paragraphs here.

## The Source Base

The research literature cited here was assembled from the NASA Technical Reports Server, from Defense Technical Information Center records reached through their registered identifiers, and from the Crossref index, across three harvests. The first served the draft, the second closed gaps that only appeared once the equations were written down, and the third served this survey. The pool stands at 4,618 records, of which 760 are NASA documents, 712 are Defense Technical Information Center reports, and the remainder are journal articles.

**The subject has a deep period record and it took two attempts to reach it.** Twenty-eight percent of the pool predates 1976, which reflects that lifting entry was worked out in a concentrated burst between about 1958 and 1972 and then largely left alone until the current revival. **The gaps were never in the archive.** Every time a topic looked thin, the cause was a search pattern written in the wrong decade's vocabulary, and the fix was to ask for roll modulation and maneuvering range rather than for crossrange.

**It also has an unusually dirty name space, and the harvest was designed around that rather than corrected afterwards.** The programme acronyms are unusable as search terms. PRIME collides with prime numbers and prime movers, and START with the Strategic Arms Reduction Treaty and with the ordinary English verb, so no query in this harvest contains either word. Beyond the acronyms, footprint belongs to carbon accounting, recovery to economics and to waste heat, ablation to medicine and to laser materials processing, and entry to almost everything. Designing the queries to be unambiguous without the programme names meant the contamination largely never entered, and the filters that would have caught it found only a few dozen records to reject.

**One family got through anyway and it was found by reading rather than by anticipating.** Lateral motion of a vehicle is a term of art in railway dynamics and in road-vehicle lane keeping, and two such records had settled among the foundational crossrange papers, which is the least visible place for them to be. Maneuvering range turns out to name an instrumented air combat facility off the East Coast, so a pattern for it retrieves a construction quality assurance plan. Lateral range is also a term in search and detection theory. Glider, maneuvering and hovering all belong to underwater vehicles as well as to aircraft.

## Epistemic State

**Historical fact, as reported by the accessible record.** The SV-5D PRIME was built by Martin Marietta for the United States Air Force as the second half of the START programme. Three vehicles were launched on Atlas boosters from Vandenberg on 21 December 1966, 5 March 1967 and 19 April 1967. The first two were lost to recovery-system failures and the third was recovered in mid-air by a JC-130B. The demanded crossranges were zero, 1,053 kilometres and 1,143 kilometres. The vehicle was 6 feet 8 inches long and weighed 894 pounds. The surviving vehicle is at the National Museum of the United States Air Force.

**Historical fact, on the designation.** The nomenclature record shows a request for X-23A submitted on 16 November 1965 for the SV-5P, disapproved on 15 December 1965 on the stated ground that the aircraft was unmanned, and a late-1966 decision to seek X-23A for the SV-5D and X-24A for the SV-5P, of which only the second was ever submitted. **This article reports that record and does not adjudicate between it and universal usage.**

**Engineering analysis, performed here and checkable.** The equilibrium-glide crossrange relation, its closed form, the optimal bank angle of 45 degrees, the required crossrange of half the ground-track spacing, and the resulting lift-to-drag requirement of 0.978 by the closed form and 1.018 by exact integration, are all derived in this article from stated assumptions. The simultaneous solution of downrange and crossrange for the bank angle and lift-to-drag ratio is likewise derived here. The heating figures follow from the Sutton and Graves correlation applied to the equilibrium-glide density.

**Inference, and flagged as such.** That the vehicle's effective lift-to-drag ratio through the third flight's manoeuvring phase was between 1.05 and 1.18, and therefore between the two published figures rather than equal to either, is an inference from the demonstrated crossrange and downrange under the equilibrium-glide model. It holds only if the demonstrated crossrange was near the vehicle's maximum, and it is sensitive to the unpublished boost range in a way the article shows rather than hides. That the two published heat-shield thicknesses correspond to the stagnation region and to lightly heated surfaces respectively is an inference from the implied heat of ablation. That nobody submitted the X-23A request because the 1965 refusal had said unmanned vehicles need none is a reading offered by the designation reference and is not documented.

**Assumptions that everything downstream inherits.** The reference area of 1.385 square metres is scaled from the X-24A and is not published for this vehicle. The nose radius of 0.1 metres, the hypersonic drag coefficient of unity, the ablator density of 550 kilograms per cubic metre, and the parachute drag coefficient of 0.75 are all assumed. The entry speed is quoted differently by different sources and both ends of the spread are carried through the analysis.

**What the record does not settle.** The vehicle's actual maximum lift-to-drag ratio. Its actual reference area. The downrange consumed by the Atlas before entry interface, which is why the bank angle could only be bracketed and not determined. The peak deceleration and heating actually measured, which were certainly telemetered and are not in the accessible literature. And the reason the X-23A request was never sent.

## Out of Scope

The ASSET half of the START programme, which deserves its own treatment. The Atlas SLV-3 as a launch vehicle. The X-24A and X-24B, which follow in this series. The mid-air retrieval technique and its origins in satellite film return. The detailed chemistry of charring ablators. The Space Shuttle crossrange requirement's full political history, of which only the arithmetic is treated here.

## Conclusion

**PRIME asked whether a spacecraft could steer on the way home, and the answer was yes, by about a thousand kilometres.**

The number matters more than it looks. A returning vehicle needs roughly half a ground-track spacing of crossrange to reach a chosen site without waiting, and at the equator that requires a lift-to-drag ratio of 0.978 by the relation the period used and 1.018 when the approximation in that relation is removed. **A lifting body near unity is therefore not a modest capability but the threshold one**, and the difference between it and a ballistic capsule is not a matter of degree. One can come home when it chooses and the other cannot.

There is one more way to see how steeply that capability is bought. The reachable ground area scales as the crossrange half-width times the downrange depth, and those go as the square and the first power of the lift-to-drag ratio respectively, so

$$A_\text{footprint} \propto \left(\frac{L}{D}\right)^3$$

**The footprint goes as the cube.** Raising the ratio from 1.0 to 1.2 enlarges the region a vehicle can reach by a factor of 1.73, which is why twenty percent of lift-to-drag ratio was worth arguing about and why the disagreement in the sources matters.

The vehicle that demonstrated this weighed less than a motorcycle, flew three times, was lost twice on the way back, and carries a designation that was never requested for it. **All four of those facts are worth keeping.**
